
====================================================================================================
TASK 2 ? PPO REPORT HANDOFF
====================================================================================================
Required research questions:
1. How does epsilon change the fraction of updates constrained by clipping and short-fork stability?
2. When KL pressure is weakened, what changes first: reward, drift, entropy, length, or qualitative behavior?
3. How informative is learned reward as the policy moves away from the reference distribution?

What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nCell membranes control the movement of materials through a process called **osmosis**. Osmosis involves the movement of water molecules from an area of higher solute concentration to an area of lower solute concentration across a semipermeable membrane. This allows for selective permeability: certain substances can pass through easily while others cannot.\n\nb. What determines the presence of the features in 2. a?\n\nThe ability of cell membranes to control the movement of materials depends on their composition and structure. They differ in structure, function, and origin:\n\n#### Cilia:\n- **Structure**: Composed of microtubules arranged in a double helix pattern around a central shaft.\n- **Function**: Propel fluid along the cell surface in a coordinated manner.\n- **Origin**: Found in epithelial tissues lining body cavities and tubes.\n\n#### Flagella:\n- **Structure**: Also made of microtubules but arranged differently forming an asymmetric structure with two tails at either end.\n- **Function**: Used for rapid forward motion, propulsion of spermatozoa, and other complex movements.\n- **Origin**: Found in many single-celled organisms and some protozoans.\n\nIn summary, while both cilia and flagella are important for motility, they serve different purposes and are structurally distinct due to their different roles in cell movement and function.\n\n### 5. a. What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nCell membranes control the movement of materials through a process called **osmosis**. Osmosis involves the movement of water molecules from an area of higher solute concentration to an area of lower solute concentration across a semipermeable membrane. This allows for selective permeability: certain substances can pass through easily while others cannot.\n\nb. What determines the presence of the features in 2. a?\n\nThe ability of cell membranes to control the movement of materials depends on their composition and structure. They differ mainly in size, shape, and function:\n\n- **Cilia**:\n  - **Size**: Smaller than flagella.\n  - **Shape**: Usually shorter and more numerous than flagella.\n  - **Movement**: Move in a coordinated wave-like motion to propel the cell along its surface.\n  - **Function**: Often associated with sensory organs or motile systems in tissues such as respiratory tracts.\n\n- **Flagella**:\n  - **Size**: Larger and more numerous than cilia.\n  - **Shape**: Long, whip-like appendages.\n  - **Movement**: Rotate back-and-forth, creating undulating waves that propel the cell forward.\n  - **Function**: Used for propulsion in swimming or moving over surfaces; important for sperm motility and other motile activities.\n\nIn summary, cilia are generally smaller, less numerous, and perform sensing functions compared to flagella, which are larger, numerous, and used for active motility.\n\n### 5. a.           "content": "Here is a piece of text: Sending a rover to Mars is cool—but sending one to Mars along with a helicopter is even better. Yes, that is the plan for the next NASA Mars rover, scheduled for 2020. The idea is to have a driving rover that brings along a small coaxial helicopter. The helicopter will be self-powered and fly for a few minutes a day. The main advantage of the helicopter is that it can scout ahead of the rover and take pictures and stuff—maybe some epic rover selfies. But really, it should be a huge advantage over previous rovers.\nBesides being a cool Mars mission, this Mars helicopter is also perfect for some physics questions. Here are some questions and answers regarding this flying robot.\nWhy doesn't it have a tail rotor?\nThe Mars helicopter is not your traditional helicopter. For a helicopter with a single rotor (like the ones you usually see on Earth), the tail rotor is required to counteract the change in angular momentum (and the frictional torque from the air) due to the main rotor spinning. Without the tail rotor, the helicopter would spin out of control and crash—or at least get the passengers very dizzy. A coaxial helicopter has two counter rotating blades. Since the two rotors are spinning in opposite directions, the total angular momentum is zero and there is no need for torque from an extra tail rotor.\nGetting rid of the tail rotor also does something else—it saves space. You can make a smaller helicopter by using a coaxial rotor. Smaller is better—especially with the limited space on board the rover. Getting a bigger helicopter on the rover would be like trying to fit an overstuffed rolling suitcase in the overhead bin on your last cross-country flight. Also, I should point out that the Mars helicopter doesn't go back to the rover, but it does start there.\nWill a helicopter work on Mars?\nWith regards to helicopter flight, there are two big differences between Earth and Mars. First, the density of the atmosphere on Mars is significantly lower than on Earth (only about 1 percent of our atmospheric density). Second, the gravitational field is also lower on Mars (just 38 percent of the gravity on the surface of the Earth). The lower atmospheric density makes it more difficult to fly a helicopter, but the lower gravity makes it easier.\nThe real question—how do helicopters fly? In a very basic model of helicopter thrust, the rotors take air above the helicopter and throw this air down. Since the \"thrown\" air has an increase in momentum, this requires a force—which is the lift force. Also, we can imagine this mass of air is in the shape of a cylinder with the radius of this air-cylinder the same as the area of the helicopter rotors.\nThe momentum of the downward thrown \"air\" (I'm going to use air for the stuff on Mars and Earth) depends on the speed that it's moving down and the mass. But what about this mass of air? Since the thrust force depends on the rate of change of momentum, I don't actually need to know the height of this cylinder of air.However, the rate of change of air-mass does indeed depend on the size of the rotors and the density of air. It includes:\n- **Water**: The primary component that provides structural support for the cell.\n- **Matter dissolved in water**: Such as salts, sugars, amino acids, nucleic acids, etc., which are essential for cellular functions.\n- **Organelles**: Small structures within the cytoplasm such as mitochondria, chloroplasts, lysosomes, ribosomes, Golgi apparatus, endoplasmic reticulum, and nuclear envelope.\n\n### 2. a. What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nThe key feature of cell membranes that controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell is their selective permeability or selectively permeable nature. This means they allow certain substances to pass through while restricting others.\n\nb. What determines the presence of this feature in cell membranes?\n\nThis feature is determined by the composition and structure of the phospholipid bilayer forming the basic unit of cell membranes. The hydrophilic heads face the aqueous environment inside and outside the cell, while the hydrophobic tails face each other inside the membrane, thus allowing molecules with similar properties to pass through but blocking larger molecules and ions from entering or exiting.\n\n### 3. a. What cell components are required to make proteins?\n\nProteins are synthesized on ribosomes located in the rough ER (endoplasmic reticulum). What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nCell membranes control the movement of materials through a process called **osmosis**. Osmosis involves the movement of water molecules from an area of higher solute concentration to an area of lower solute concentration across a semipermeable membrane. This allows for selective permeability: certain substances can pass through easily while others cannot.\n\nb. What determines the presence of the features in 2. a?\n\nThe ability of cell membranes to control the movement of materials depends on their composition and structure. Key factors include:\n- **Proteins**: Integral proteins embedded in the membrane allow specific molecules to enter or exit the cell. These proteins often have binding sites where specific molecules attach.\n- **Phospholipids**: Form the bilayer structure of the membrane, which provides structural integrity and acts as a barrier to some molecules.\n\nThus, it’s the combination of phospholipid bilayers and integral proteins that determine how well cell membranes regulate material transport.\n\n### 3. a. Compare and contrast cilia and flagella.\n\nCilia and flagella are both hairlike structures found in various eukaryotic cells, used for locomotion and sensory reception. They differ in structure, function, and origin:\n\n#### Cilia:\n- **Structure**: Composed of microtubules arranged in a double helix pattern around a central shaft.\n- **Function**: Propel fluid along the cell surface in a coordinated manner, helping with respiration, circulation, and other bodily functions.\n- **Origin**: Found throughout the body except in the brain and spinal cord, they originate from basal bodies located at the base of the cell.\n\n#### Flagella:\n- **Structure**: Similar to cilia but elongated and thinner, consisting mainly of microtubules without a central shaft.\n- **Function**: Provide propulsion, facilitating movement away from the body surface.\n- **Origin**: Also found in most animal cells but less frequently seen in plants; they arise from centrioles in the cell center.\n\nIn summary, while both structures facilitate movement, cilia are shorter and more numerous, whereas flagella are longer and fewer in number.\n\n### 5. a. For a helicopter with a single rotor (like the ones you usually see on Earth), the tail rotor is required to counteract the change in angular momentum (and the frictional torque from the air) due to the main rotor spinning. Without the tail rotor, the helicopter would spin out of control and crash—or at least get the passengers very dizzy. A coaxial helicopter has two counter rotating blades. Since the two rotors are spinning in opposite directions, the total angular momentum is zero and there is no need for torque from an extra tail rotor.\nGetting rid of the tail rotor also does something else—it saves space. You can make a smaller helicopter by using a coaxial rotor. Smaller is better—especially with the limited space on board the rover. Getting a bigger helicopter on the rover would be like trying to fit an overstuffed rolling suitcase in the overhead bin on your last cross-country flight. Also, I should point out that the Mars helicopter doesn't go back to the rover, but it does start there.\nWill a helicopter work on Mars?\nWith regards to helicopter flight, there are two big differences between Earth and Mars. First, the density of the atmosphere on Mars is significantly lower than on Earth (only about 1 percent of our atmospheric density). Second, the gravitational field is also lower on Mars (just 38 percent of the gravity on the surface of the Earth). The lower atmospheric density makes it more difficult to fly a helicopter, but the lower gravity makes it easier.\nThe real question—how do helicopters fly? In a very basic model of helicopter thrust, the rotors take air above the helicopter and throw this air down. Since the \"thrown\" air has an increase in momentum, this requires a force—which is the lift force. Also, we can imagine this mass of air is in the shape of a cylinder with the radius of this air-cylinder the same as the area of the helicopter rotors.\nThe momentum of the downward thrown \"air\" (I'm going to use air for the stuff on Mars and Earth) depends on the speed that it's moving down and the mass. But what about this mass of air? Since the thrust force depends on the rate of change of momentum, I don't actually need to know the height of this cylinder of air.However, the rate of change of air-mass does indeed depend on the size of the rotors and the density of air. From that, I can get the following rough approximation for the thrust force from a helicopter.\nYou can probably guess that the A is the rotor area and v is the speed the air is pushed down. The density of air is represented by the Greek letter ρ. But what about gravity? Well, if the helicopter is to hover then the thrust force must be equal to the weight. We can calculate the weight as the product of mass (m) and gravitational field (g).\nSince the air density on Mars is just 0.01 that of on Earth but the gravitational field is 0.38 as much, the lower gravity benefit does not match the loss in air density. It's more difficult to hover on Mars than on Earth. Oh, please notice that this is just a rough model for the thrust force from a helicopter. Please do not use this calculation to attempt to build your own human-carrying helicopter. However, you could use it to calculate the correct size of the S.H.I.E.L.D. helicarrier rotors.\nActually, here is a quick homework question for you. The rest of the time, it will use its solar panel on the top of the vehicle to recharge the battery. So, how big of a battery does this thing need? There are actually two ways to estimate the battery size (other than asking NASA—that's the boring way).\nThe first method considers the charging time. Basically, this thing will recharge for a whole day. On Mars, each day is almost exactly the same as an Earth day (but 37 minutes longer). But the total day doesn't matter—what matters is the length of sunlight time. Just like on Earth, the length of daylight on Mars changes with the seasons. So, let's just estimate that the sun is visible of 10 hours a day (I just picked that for fun).\nThe next thing to determine is to think about the solar irradiance. This is the power per unit area that the is in the light reaching the surface of the planet. For Earth, this has a maximum of about 1,000 Watts per square meter. On Mars, it's only about 2590 Watts/m2—but remember that is the maximum. Over the course of a day, the sun appears to change position. If you have a static facing solar panel, the average irradiance will be smaller. I'm just going to go with 295 Watt/m2. Since the size of the solar panel appears to be approximately the size of the cube part of the robot, I will estimate its radius (it looks circular) at 7 cm. With this size, irradiance, length of a day, and an efficiency of 25 percent, that would give a total energy of about 40,000 Joules. That's very close to the energy stored in the battery of the iPhone X—note, there are some tricks going from units of mAh to Joules.\nNow for the second method. How much power would it take for the Mars helicopter to fly for three minutes? How do you calculate flying power? Well, think of it this way. Power is the rate at which you use energy. What is the helicopter doing with its energy? It is taking air above it and increasing its speed (an increase in kinetic energy). I'm skipping some steps, but from that I can get an expression for the power needed to hover—you can find all the details in my calculation of the power needed for a human propelled helicopter (yes, that's a real thing).\nTo calculate the flying power, I need to know the rotor size (14 cm diameter) and the helicopter mass (about 1 kg). From this, I can calculate the thrust air speed needed to hover. And with the speed, I can calculate the rate of change of air kinetic energy—which is the power. With these values (along with the density of air and the gravitational field on Mars), I get a flying power of 374 Watts (note: here are my calculations in python).\nBut wait. Acknowledging their moral aspirations might better encourage buy-in to educators’ messages,” Wahl suggested.\nMoreover, she suggested that officers need practical coaching in how to do their jobs without using torture.\n“They know that it is illegal, and they know that human rights activists think it is wrong.           "baseline_response": "To determine the average shear stress acting on any cross-section of a simply supported beam subjected to a point load, we can use the formula for shear stress:\n\n\\[\n\\tau = \\frac{M}{I}\n\\]\n\nwhere:\n- \\(\\tau\\) is the shear stress,\n- \\(M\\) is the bending moment at that section,\n- \\(I\\) is the moment of inertia about the neutral axis.\n\nFor a simply supported beam with a point load (applied load) at its midpoint, there are two moments acting at the midspan:\n\n1. **First Moment** (\\(M_1\\)): This is due to the distributed load over half the span.\n2. **Second Moment** (\\(M_2\\)): This is due to the concentrated load at the midpoint.\n\n### Step-by-Step Calculation\n\n#### 1. Determine the Distributed Load:\nThe total load is 10,000 lb, and it acts uniformly across the entire length of the beam (10 ft). Therefore, the distributed load per foot is:\n\n\\[\nq = \\frac{10,000 \\text{ lb}}{10 \\text{ ft}} = 1,000 \\text{ lb/ft}\n\\]\n\n#### 2. Calculate the First Moment (Moment Due to Distributed Load):\nThe first moment occurs when the load is spread out equally over the remaining part of the beam after accounting for the applied load.\n\nThe moment arm \\(a\\) from the left support to the center is:\n\n\\[\na = \\frac{\\text{Beam Length} - \\text{Point Load Distance}}{2} = \\frac{10 \\text{ ft} - 5 \\text{ ft}}{2} = 2.5 \\text{ ft}\n\\]\n\nThe first moment’s moment \\(M_1\\) is given by:\n\n\\[\nM_1 = q \\times a^2 = 1,000 \\text{ lb/ft} \\times (2.5 \\text{ ft})^2 = 6,250 \\text{ lb-ft}\n\\]\n\n#### 3. Calculate the Second Moment (Concentrated Load):\nThe second moment’s moment \\(M_2\\) is simply the value of the concentrated load at the midpoint:\n\n\\[\nM_2 = 10,000 \\text{ lb}\n\\]\n\n#### 4. Combine the Moments to Find Total Bending Moment at Midspan:\nAt midspan, the total bending moment \\(M\\) is the sum of these two moments:\n\n\\[\nM = M_1 + M_2 = 6,250 \\text{ lb-ft} + 10,000 \\text{ lb} = 16,250 \\text{ lb-ft}\n\\]\n\n#### 5. Calculate the Moment of Inertia (I):\nGiven the cross-sectional area \\(A\\) is 1 square inch, which equals \\(0.0001\\) cubic inches (since \\(1 \\text{ in}^2 = 0.0001 \\text{ in}^3\\)).\n\nUsing the parallel axis theorem, the moment of inertia \\(I\\) for an I-beam or rectangular section about the neutral axis can be approximated using the following relationship:\n\n\\[\nI = A \\cdot b \\cdot h^2 / 12\n\\]\n\nWhere \\(b\\) is the width and \\(h\\) is the height of the section. However, without specific dimensions provided, let's assume a typical size for an I-beam section, such as:\n\n- Width \\(b = 0.5 \\text{ in}\\)\n- Height \\(h = 0.7",
          "comparison_response": "To solve this problem, we need to analyze the loading and distribution of shear force across the cross-section of the beam.\n\n### Given Data:\n- Beam length (L) = 10 feet\n- Applied load (P) = 10,000 lb\n- Cross-sectional area (A) = 1 square inch\n\n### Step-by-Step Solution:\n\n#### 1. **Calculate the Shear Force at Different Points**\n   - Since the beam is simply supported and there is an applied load \\( P \\) in the middle, we can divide the beam into two halves.\n   - At the point where the load \\( P \\) is applied, the shear force will be zero because no external forces are acting on that particular section.\n\n   For simplicity, let’s consider the half-beam from the left support to the point of application of the load \\( P \\).\n\n   In the first half of the beam (left side), since it is simply supported and has only the applied load \\( P \\), the shear force will increase linearly with distance from the point of application until it reaches its maximum value at the end of the beam (right support).\n\n   Let's denote:\n   - \\( V_L \\) as the shear force just before reaching the load \\( P \\).\n   - \\( V_R \\) as the shear force just after passing the load \\( P \\).\n\n   Using Hooke's Law for beams under uniformly distributed loads, the shear force increases linearly with the load. Therefore:\n\n   \\[\n   V_L = \\frac{P}{2}\n   \\]\n   \n   \\[\n   V_R = \\frac{3P}{4} \n   \\]\n\n#### 2. **Determine Average Shear Stress**\n\nThe average shear stress (\\(\\tau_{avg}\\)) over the entire cross-section is given by the formula:\n\n\\[\n\\tau_{avg} = \\frac{\\text{Shear Force}}{\\text{Cross-sectional Area}}\n\\]\n\nGiven:\n- Shear Force at the start (\\(V_L\\)): \\( V_L = \\frac{P}{2} = \\frac{10,000 \\text{ lb}}{2} = 5,000 \\text{ lb} \\)\n- Cross-sectional area (A): \\( A = 1 \\text{ in}^2 \\)\n\nTherefore,\n\n\\[\n\\tau_{avg} = \\frac{5,000 \\text{ lb}}{1 \\text{ in}^2} = 5,000 \\text{ psi}\n\\]\n\n### Conclusion:\n\nThe average shear stress acting on any cross section of the beam is \\( 5,000 \\text{ psi} \\). Here are some questions and answers regarding this flying robot.\nWhy doesn't it have a tail rotor?\nThe Mars helicopter is not your traditional helicopter. For a helicopter with a single rotor (like the ones you usually see on Earth), the tail rotor is required to counteract the change in angular momentum (and the frictional torque from the air) due to the main rotor spinning. Without the tail rotor, the helicopter would spin out of control and crash—or at least get the passengers very dizzy. A coaxial helicopter has two counter rotating blades. Since the two rotors are spinning in opposite directions, the total angular momentum is zero and there is no need for torque from an extra tail rotor.\nGetting rid of the tail rotor also does something else—it saves space. You can make a smaller helicopter by using a coaxial rotor. Smaller is better—especially with the limited space on board the rover. Second, the gravitational field is also lower on Mars (just 38 percent of the gravity on the surface of the Earth). The lower atmospheric density makes it more difficult to fly a helicopter, but the lower gravity makes it easier.\nThe real question—how do helicopters fly? This comprehensive analysis will examine how pesticides affect different types of wildlife, explore their long-term ecological impacts, and propose viable alternative methods for pest control.\n\n### Impact on Wildlife\n\n#### Birds:\n- **Effects:** Pesticides often target pests that birds eat or nest around. Exposure can lead to reproductive failures, deformities, and mortality.\n- **Examples:** DDT has caused numerous cases of eggshell thinning in many bird species, leading to increased vulnerability to predators and reduced offspring numbers.\n\n#### Fish:\n- **Effects:** Organophosphate insecticides, such as malathion, can bioaccumulate in fish tissues, causing neurological damage and decreased fertility.\n- **Examples:** Studies show that exposure to these chemicals can reduce fish populations and alter aquatic ecosystems.\n\n#### Bees:\n- **Effects:** Neonicotinoids, like clothianidin and imidacloprid, have been linked to colony collapse disorder in bees, which is a serious threat to pollination services.\n- **Examples:** Research indicates that these pesticides can weaken bee immunity, impair learning ability, and disrupt critical developmental stages.\n\n#### Amphibians:\n- **Effects:** Chlordane and heptachlor are persistent organochlorine pesticides that have severe toxic effects on amphibians, reducing population sizes and altering genetic diversity.\n- **Examples:** These toxins accumulate in amphibian tissues over time, making them more susceptible to other diseases and contributing to mass extinctions.\n\n### Long-Term Ecological Impacts\n\nThe cumulative effect of widespread pesticide use can lead to complex ecological disruptions. For instance:\n\n- **Habitat Loss:** Pesticide residues can contaminate soil and water bodies, rendering areas uninhabitable for native plants and animals.\n- **Ecosystem Disruption:** Changes in predator-prey relationships due to pesticide-induced declines in prey populations can cascade through entire food chains.\n- **Genetic Variability:** Chronic stress from pesticide exposure may contribute to genetic mutations, further compromising biodiversity.\n\n### Sustainable Alternatives\n\n#### Integrated Pest Management (IPM):\n- **Definition:** IPM combines biological, physical, cultural, and chemical controls tailored to specific conditions.\n- **Effectiveness:** Proven effective in maintaining healthy environments while minimizing harm to beneficial organisms.\n- **Example:** Using natural predators like ladybugs to control aphids without relying solely on chemical treatments.\n\n#### Biological Control:\n- **Methods:** Employing insects that naturally feed on pests.\n- **Effectiveness:** Highly targeted and environmentally friendly; e.g., using parasitic wasps to control apple maggot flies.\n- **Potential Drawbacks:** May require continuous monitoring and management efforts.\n\n#### Cultural Practices:\n- **Methods:** Adjusting farming techniques based on crop rotations and habitat management.\n- **Effectiveness:** Enhances soil health and reduces reliance on synthetic chemicals.\n- **Example:** Crop rotation reduces pest pressure by alternating crops, thereby breaking pest life cycles.\n\n#### Physical Controls:\n- **Methods:** Utilizing barriers and traps to prevent pests from entering gardens or buildings.\n- **Effectiveness:** Simple yet highly effective when combined with other strategies.\n- **Example:** Installing row covers to protect plants against pests before planting season begins.\n\n### Conclusion\n\nWhile pesticides offer immediate relief from pest infestations, their indiscriminate application poses grave risks to wildlife and the environment.       "response": "Sure! Let's address each question one by one:\n\n### 1. What does the protoplasm of a cell include?\n\nThe protoplasm of a cell is primarily made up of:\n- **Cytoplasm**: The jelly-like substance inside the cell that contains organelles like ribosomes, mitochondria, and the nucleus.\n- **Nucleus**: Contains genetic material (DNA) essential for cellular functions.\n- **Organelles**: Tiny structures within the cytoplasm with specialized functions such as photosynthesis in chloroplasts or protein synthesis in ribosomes.\n\nSo, the protoplasm includes not just water but also organelles and genetic information necessary for life processes.\n\n### 2. a. What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nCell membranes control the movement of materials through a process called **osmosis**. Osmosis involves the movement of water molecules from an area of higher solute concentration to an area of lower solute concentration across a semipermeable membrane. This allows for selective permeability: certain substances can pass through easily while others cannot.\n\nb. What determines the presence of the features in 2. a?\n\nThe ability of cell membranes to control the movement of materials depends on their composition and structure. Key factors include:\n- **Proteins**: Integral proteins embedded in the membrane allow specific molecules to enter or exit the cell. These proteins often have binding sites where specific molecules attach.\n- **Phospholipids**: Form the bilayer structure of the membrane, which provides structural integrity and acts as a barrier to some molecules.\n\nThus, it’s the combination of phospholipid bilayers and integral proteins that determine how well cell membranes regulate material transport.\n\n### 3. a. What cell components are required to make proteins?\n\nTo synthesize proteins, cells require several key components including:\n- **Ribosomes**: Site where proteins are assembled using amino acids.\n- **Genetic Material (DNA)**: Information encoding the sequence of amino acids in proteins.\n- **Amino Acids**: Building blocks of proteins.\n- **mRNA**: Transcription factor carrying the genetic code from DNA to ribosomes.\n- **tRNAs**: Transfer RNA carrying specific amino acids to the ribosome.\n\nWithout these components, cells would be unable to produce proteins.\n\n### 4. Compare and contrast cilia and flagella.\n\nCilia and flagella are both hair-like structures found in eukaryotic cells used for locomotion and signal transduction. They differ mainly in size, shape, and function:\n\n- **Cilia**:\n  - **Size**: Smaller than flagella.\n  - **Shape**: Usually shorter and more numerous than flagella.\n  - **Movement**: Move in a coordinated wave-like motion to propel the cell along its surface.\n  - **Function**: Often associated with sensory organs or motile systems in tissues such as respiratory tracts.\n\n- **Flagella**:\n  - **Size**: Larger and more numerous than cilia.\n  - **Shape**: Long, whip-like appendages.\n  - **Movement**: Rotate back-and-forth, creating undulating waves that propel the cell forward.\n  - **Function**: Used for propulsion in swimming or moving over surfaces; important for sperm motility and other motile activities.\n\nIn summary, cilia are generally smaller, less numerous, and perform sensing functions compared to flagella, which are larger, numerous, and used for active motility.\n\n### 5. a. For a helicopter with a single rotor (like the ones you usually see on Earth), the tail rotor is required to counteract the change in angular momentum (and the frictional torque from the air) due to the main rotor spinning. Since the two rotors are spinning in opposite directions, the total angular momentum is zero and there is no need for torque from an extra tail rotor.\nGetting rid of the tail rotor also does something else—it saves space. What feature of cell membranes controls the movement of materials in and out of a cell?\n\nCell membranes control the movement of materials through a process called **osmosis**. Osmosis involves the movement of water molecules from an area of higher solute concentration to an area of lower solute concentration across a semipermeable membrane. This allows for selective permeability: certain substances can pass through easily while others cannot.\n\nb. What determines the presence of the features in 2. a?\n\nThe ability of cell membranes to control the movement of materials depends on their composition and structure. Key factors include:\n- **Proteins**: Integral proteins embedded in the membrane allow specific molecules to enter or exit the cell. These proteins often have specific binding sites for particular substances.\n- **Phospholipids**: Form the bilayer structure of the membrane, which provides structural integrity and acts as a barrier to some molecules.\n\nThus, it’s the combination of phospholipid bilayers and integral proteins that determine how well cell membranes regulate material transport.\n\n### 3. a. They differ mainly in size, shape, and function:\n\n- **Cilia**:\n  - **Size**: Smaller than flagella.\n  - **Shape**: Usually shorter and more numerous than flagella.\n  - **Movement**: Move in a coordinated wave-like motion to propel the cell along its surface.\n  - **Function**: Often associated with sensory organs or motile systems in tissues such as respiratory tracts.\n\n- **Flagella**:\n  - **Size**: Larger and more numerous than cilia.\n  - **Shape**: Long, whip-like appendages.\n  - **Movement**: Rotate back-and-forth, creating rapid, directed movements.\n  - **Function**: Used in swimming, moving around surfaces, and initiating muscle contractions.\n\nIn summary, cilia are typically smaller and less numerous, move slowly compared to flagella, and serve roles related to sensory input and surface propulsion, whereas flagella are larger, rotate rapidly, and are central to active movement and muscular contraction in many organisms.\n\n### 5. a. ====================================================================================================
21. REQUIRED INTERPRETATION WARNINGS
====================================================================================================
- Sampled token KL can be slightly negative because it is a sampled log-ratio estimate; true KL is non-negative.
- Reward-model score is appropriate for controlled within-task comparisons but is not calibrated overall response quality.
- Entropy is a confidence/diversity diagnostic, not a quality measure.
- PPO clipping epsilon constrains movement relative to the rollout policy during an update.
- KL beta penalizes movement away from the separate frozen reference policy.
- The report requires one qualitative case where reward and response quality agree and one where they disagree.
- Reward overoptimization should not be claimed from reward alone; it requires response-quality evidence.
